Nobody is beaming any of that into my head, I promise you. I've earned every single one of those headaches through two decades on the sales floor. \n\nAnd as for taking them out... well, sometimes I wish someone would take a few out of my mind, to be perfectly honest. Like when I'm trying to sleep and I keep remembering the back stockroom is a mess and the seasonal aisle needs a complete reset before Sunday. But no, they stay right up there, locked in tight. It's just my own brain, doing its own thing, completely isolated. It's just... like I said, it's the exit door that's broken. The translation from my brain to my mouth. So, no, nothing sci-fi like what you're describing. Just me and the inventory in there.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something strongly that turned out not to be true, even though you were completely sure it was real at the time?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that wasn't true? No, Doc, I can't say that I have. I'm a pretty grounded guy. Honestly, you have to be in retail. You can't just make things up or believe in fairy tales when you've got a district manager breathing down your neck about shrink percentages and quarter-to-date sales, you know? \n\nI mean, if I start believing we have three pallets of paper towels in the back when we actually only have one, that's a real problem. That messes up the whole replenishment cycle. So my brain is very much tied to the facts. The physical boxes, the numbers on the spreadsheet, the actual clock-in times for my cashiers. I deal in what's right in front of me. \n\nNow, I've had employees who believe some wild things, let me tell you. I had a kid last summer—nice kid, good at facing the shelves—but he was absolutely convinced that corporate was sending secret shoppers in every single Tuesday just to watch him specifically. I had to sit him down in the breakroom, right next to that vending machine that always eats the quarters—which is a whole other issue I've been trying to get the vendor to fix since April, but they never return my calls—and I had to tell him, \"Look, son, nobody from corporate cares enough to spy on you. They barely care enough to send us the right promotional signage.\" \n\nBut me? No, I don't get into all that. I know what's real and what isn't. My problem isn't what I believe, it's just... like I keep saying, it's how it all comes out of my mouth when I try to explain it to people. I'm perfectly clear on the reality of a situation. I just can't always get the words to match up with that reality once I start talking. So no, I've never had a situation where I was completely sure of something that turned out to be fake. I stick to the inventory.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, didn't... didn't we just cover this? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing a couple of minutes ago. Maybe my words got all jumbled up again and I didn't make myself clear, which, again, is the whole reason I'm here. But like I was saying, no. Not even once. 

If people could hear my thoughts, believe me, I would have been fired from the company twenty years ago. Just yesterday, for example, we had this woman come in to the service desk trying to return a blender. And I'm looking at it, and it clearly has dried margarita mix crusted all over the base, completely sticky, no box, no receipt. She's standing there demanding a full cash refund, and I'm doing the whole "the customer is always right" routine, smiling and nodding. But in my head? In my head, I'm thinking, "Lady, you used this for a weekend tailgate party and now you expect my store to eat the cost." 

If she could hear my thoughts without me speaking them, or if someone else was putting thoughts in my head right then, that whole interaction would have been an absolute disaster. Corporate would have had my head on a platter. So it's a very good thing that it's totally private up there. It is just me, my own thoughts, locked away where nobody can get to them. 

I don't know why you're asking me this one again, I really don't. Maybe it's a standard checklist thing you have to get through, like when corporate makes us do those quarterly safety audits and we have to check the fire extinguishers even though we literally just checked them the month before. I get it, you have your processes. But I can promise you, on my life, my thoughts are my own. Nobody is listening in. It's just me in there, thinking about the store, thinking about our profit margins, and thinking about how I need to get the HVAC guy out to fix the air conditioning in the breakroom before July hits, because those nineteen-year-olds will absolutely mutiny if they have to eat their lunch in an eighty-degree room. So, no. Nothing like that at all.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been troubling you and what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain, but I'll try to lay it all out for you because I really need someone to understand what's been happening at the store. I've been a supervisor in retail for years, you know, managing the floor, handling the inventory, making sure the weekend schedules are tight. But lately, when I try to explain the inventory numbers to my team, or even just go over the weekend schedule, which should be simple, right? It's just names and times. But my words... they just start jumping around. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks we're expecting, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, because Thursday is when the big shipment comes, but then that makes me think about the church grill we used last Thursday for the community event, and then somehow I'm talking about my boss's tone of voice when he asked about the grill. And I can see it on their faces. The team, they just stand there, looking at me like they can't follow a single thing I'm saying. Like I'm speaking a different language or something. And I'm trying to be clear, I really am, because as a supervisor, you have to be clear, but the thoughts just keep sliding into each other, from the trucks to the Thursdays to the grill to the boss, and I just can't seem to make them line up straight anymore. It's incredibly frustrating, and honestly, a little frightening, which is why I decided I had to come in and see you today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else, taken out of your mind, or broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my thoughts are definitely my own, you know? They're mine. It's just that there are so many of them, and they move so fast, like a rushing river, just washing over everything. But nobody is putting them there. I've never felt like... like someone is inserting thoughts into my head. I know some people talk about that, you see it in movies or read about it, but for me, it's just my own brain going into overdrive. And they aren't being taken out either. If anything, I wish someone would take a few of them out, just to give me some peace and quiet! Because when I'm standing there on the floor, trying to figure out where to put the new seasonal display, and I'm thinking about the display, and then the lighting, and then the electric bill, and then the guy who came to fix the meter last month... they're all my thoughts. They just won't stop piling up. \n\nAnd as for people hearing them... well, no, not hearing them like a radio broadcast or anything. They can't hear the thoughts themselves. They just hear the jumble that comes out of my mouth when I try to explain it all. I can see them trying to listen, trying to catch the thread, but the thread is tangled up with ten other threads. So they don't hear my thoughts, they just hear the mess I make when I try to speak them. But the thoughts themselves? No, they stay locked right up here in my own head, for better or worse. Mostly worse lately, to be honest. It's just... it's just me in here, dealing with all this noise.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing, hearing, or feeling things that others around you don’t seem to notice, or have you had beliefs that turn out to be quite different from what others believe?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, if you're asking if I see things that aren't there, or hear voices whispering to me, no. The store is loud enough as it is, you know? Between the overhead announcements—which, by the way, the speaker in aisle four has been crackling for three months and corporate still hasn't sent anyone to fix it, despite me putting in four separate work orders—and the constant hum of the refrigeration units in the back, I hear plenty of real things. Too many real things, maybe! But they're definitely there. Anyone walking in can hear that awful crackle. And the music, they play this same loop of 80s pop hits, day in, day out. Sometimes I think that alone is going to drive me crazy, just hearing that same Wham! song for the thousandth time while I'm trying to balance the register tills at closing. But it's a real song. The cashiers complain about it just as much as I do. We all hear it. \n\nAnd as for beliefs... no, I wouldn't say I have any strange beliefs, or things that other people disagree with me on, not in the way you mean. I mean, I believe in working hard, I believe in showing up on time—which is more than I can say for some of the new hires we've had lately, let me tell you. Just last week, I had a kid, twenty-two years old, college degree, but he actually thought it was perfectly fine to clock in fifteen minutes late just because he couldn't find a parking spot right up front. I had to sit him down in the breakroom and explain that the schedule is the schedule, and if you know parking is bad on a Saturday, you leave your house twenty minutes earlier. But that's just normal, right? That's just common sense and how you run a business. I don't have any wild theories about the world or the government or anything like that. \n\nIt's really just the talking, the way my words get all tangled up when I'm trying to explain the schedule or the inventory to those kids, like I was saying before. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary. I see exactly what everyone else sees—the cardboard boxes, the empty shelves when the truck is late, the customers getting impatient... it's just that my brain tries to process all of those real things at the exact same time, and then I can't get the sentences out straight. But no, no invisible things. Just the regular, everyday headaches of retail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately—like feeling unusually down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or on the flip side, feeling unusually energetic, restless, or overly confident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say my mood has changed. I'm not... I'm not sitting around feeling hopeless or anything like that. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, like I was saying, because of the whole talking thing, but that's just a normal reaction, right? If you couldn't explain a simple weekend schedule to your team, you'd be frustrated too. But I haven't lost interest in my life or my hobbies. I still look forward to my days off. I've got this little patio garden out back—mostly heirloom tomatoes, a few jalapeños—and I still get out there every Sunday morning. The aphids were terrible this past summer, just absolutely relentless, but I kept at it with the neem oil, checking the undersides of the leaves, pruning the dead stems... I still enjoy all of that. I still like grilling on the weekends, watching the game. I haven't lost my spark for the things I like to do. \n\nAnd as for the other side of it, feeling overly energetic or... what did you say, restless? Overly confident? Ha! Doctor, I'm fifty-nine years old and I spend fifty hours a week walking up and down on a hard linoleum floor. By the time I clock out and get to my car, my knees are aching and my feet are absolutely barking. I am definitely not bouncing off the walls or feeling like I can take on the world. I just want to get home, take my shoes off, sit in my recliner, and watch the local news. I don't have any grand ideas about being the CEO of the company or anything like that. I just want to do my job, which I've always been good at. I've got a bachelor's degree, I know how retail works inside and out, I know how to manage people. \n\nIt's just... it's just this one specific thing, the way my thoughts slide together when I talk. That's the only thing bothering me. If I could just get my mouth to cooperate with my brain like it used to, everything would be perfectly fine. But my mood itself? No, my mood is exactly the same as it's always been. Just steady. Just regular old me, trying to get through the week.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble getting your thoughts organized to speak, do your thoughts ever feel disconnected from you, like they're not yours, or like someone else is influencing how you think?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just like I was saying earlier. They don't feel disconnected from me at all. I mean, they are entirely mine. It’s not like... it’s not like there’s some puppet master pulling the strings or someone beaming instructions into my head. When I get stuck, like when I’m having that trouble getting the words organized... it’s actually the exact opposite of feeling disconnected. I feel almost too connected to every single detail. \n\nTake Tuesday, for example. I was trying to explain the new endcap display for the back-to-school promotion to Sarah—she's one of our newer shift leads, sweet girl, but needs a lot of direction. Anyway, I knew exactly what I wanted to say. I had the whole plan right here in my head. I was thinking about the binders going on the top shelf, the highlighters on the pegs, the promotional signage that came in bent from the distributor so I had to tape it up in the back room... and these are all my own thoughts, right? Based on my own experience. I know merchandising. But when I opened my mouth to just tell her, \"Hey Sarah, put the binders on the top shelf,\" my brain also tried to tell her about the tape on the sign, and the inventory count for the highlighters, and the fact that corporate sent the wrong SKU for the spiral notebooks. \n\nIt all just jams up. Like a... like a ten-car pileup right at the exit ramp of my mouth. I’m standing there, fully aware that I’m making a mess of the instructions, and I can see her looking at me like I’m speaking Greek, but I can't stop the words from tangling together. So, no, nobody else is influencing how I think. It’s just my own brain trying to process fifty real, actual things about the store at the exact same time, and failing to filter it down into one simple sentence. It’s incredibly frustrating, I'll tell you that much, but it's one hundred percent just me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt so down or depressed that you lost interest in most things, or had trouble functioning for several days or more, or conversely, a time when you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: No, no, I know you're trying to be thorough, Doc, and I appreciate that, I really do, but I think that's pretty much what we were just talking about, right? My mood is fine. It’s always been fine. I’ve never had a time where I couldn’t function or just stayed in bed for days. Good lord, if I stayed in bed for several days, the store would absolutely fall apart. \n\nI mean, I remember back in, oh, it must have been 2018, right before Black Friday. Both of my assistant managers caught that terrible stomach flu that was going around the county. Just completely wiped them out. And my wife—well, ex-wife now—had just moved out that exact same month. A lot of guys might have thrown in the towel right then and there, pulled the covers over their heads, you know, got depressed and just stopped functioning. But not me. I didn't lose interest in my life. I was there at 4:30 in the morning, unlocking the front doors, making sure the promotional pallets were dropped in the main action alley before the crowds showed up. I functioned just fine, better than fine, really. I worked sixty-five hours that week and still managed to get my oil changed on Sunday. So no, no periods of being too down to live my life. I've always been a doer. \n\nAnd the sleep thing? Not needing sleep or feeling unusually high? Listen, if I don't get my seven hours, I am completely useless. I am in bed by 10:15 every single night, like clockwork. I watch the 10 o'clock local news, I wait for Jim's weather report—he's the meteorologist on channel four, been there for maybe fifteen years, always gets the winter snow accumulation wrong but what can you do, he's a staple—and as soon as Jim says goodnight, I click off the TV and I am out cold. I need that sleep because, like I said, being on your feet all day at fifty-nine takes a toll on the joints. I've never had a time where I felt like I was running on some magical battery that wouldn't die, or where I had so much energy I didn't know what to do with it. I get tired just like everybody else. I wake up, I drink my coffee—just regular drip coffee, black, nothing fancy—and I go to work. \n\nIt’s always been a very steady routine. Just a steady, normal, predictable life. That's why this whole... this whole traffic jam in my head when I try to speak is so bothersome. Because everything else about me, my energy, my mood, my sleep, it's all exactly the same as it was ten, twenty years ago. It's just the words that won't line up right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time in your life when you felt extremely down or depressed for at least two weeks, or had a period where you felt unusually energetic, powerful, or didn’t need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like we're going in circles a bit here, aren't we? I mean, you asked me about being down, and then about being depressed, and now extremely down for two weeks... I promise you, my answer hasn't changed. I really don't have these mood swings you're looking for. \n\nTwo weeks is a long time, and I can tell you exactly what my life looks like for any two-week period you want to pick out of a calendar. Take the first two weeks of November, for instance, right before the holiday retail rush really kicks into high gear. I'm doing the floor sets, I'm checking the stockroom, I'm making sure the seasonal hires actually know how to use the POS registers—which, let me tell you, corporate updates the software on those things every three years and it never gets any more intuitive. During those two weeks, am I tired? Yes. Am I stressed about getting everything done? Sure. But depressed? Down in the dumps? No. I just put my head down and do the work. I've never had a two-week stretch where I just couldn't function or felt extremely down. It's just not in my nature.\n\nAnd as for feeling powerful or... or unusually energetic? Doc, I'm a retail supervisor. I'm not a politician, I'm not a king. The most power I have is deciding who gets the Saturday morning shift and who has to close the store on Sunday night. It's just a job. I certainly don't walk around feeling like Superman or like I have some kind of special energy. I just feel like a fifty-nine-year-old guy whose feet hurt. \n\nAnd the sleep thing, I mean, I really can't stress this enough, because it's like my body has a strict internal clock. If I try to skip out on sleep, if I get, say, five hours instead of my usual seven, I get this terrible dull headache right behind my eyes for the entire next day. I can't function like that. So no, there has never been a time in my life where I felt like I didn't need sleep. I need every single minute of it. \n\nI really think we're barking up the wrong tree with all these questions about my mood. My mood is a flat line. Just steady. It's just the talking. The way the words pile up and jam together when I try to explain something simple. That's the only thing that's wrong with me. Everything else is just... just regular old life.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something is true that others don't seem to agree with, even though you're sure it's real?\n\nPatient: You mean like... like conspiracies or something? Barcodes, SKUs, inventory counts, planograms. If corporate sends down a directive that the summer clearance needs to be marked down by forty percent on Thursday, then that's the reality. There's no hidden meaning to it, you know? It's just retail. \n\nNow, if you're asking if I disagree with people about things I know are true... well, sure, but just regular everyday work stuff. Like, take our district manager, Greg. Greg is absolutely convinced we can run the entire Saturday afternoon rush with only three cashiers and one floor associate. I know for a fact that between 1:00 and 4:00 PM, we have lines backing up all the way into the juniors department. I know exactly what's going on around me at all times, probably better than most of the kids working under me. It's just... like I keep trying to explain, it's just the delivery system. The way the thoughts stack up and jam the exit when I try to speak. That's the only disconnect. Everything makes sense to me. I'll be standing there, say, in the morning huddle by the registers, and I'll try to explain something completely standard. And I'll stop and look at my associates, and people are just looking at me like they absolutely can't follow what I'm saying. They get this blank, sort of worried stare, like I'm speaking Greek. So, no, nobody else is influencing how I think. It’s incredibly frustrating, I'll tell you that much, but it's one hundred percent just me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt so down or depressed that you lost interest in most things, or had trouble functioning for several days or more, or conversely, a time when you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: No, no, I know you're trying to be thorough, Doc, and I appreciate that, I really do, but I think that's pretty much what we were just talking about, right? My mood is fine. It’s always been fine. I’ve never had a time where I couldn’t function or just stayed in bed for days. Good lord, if I stayed in bed for several days, the store would absolutely fall apart. \n\nI mean, I remember back in, oh, it must have been 2018, right before Black Friday. Both of my assistant managers caught that terrible stomach flu that was going around the county. Just completely wiped them out. And my wife—well, ex-wife now—had just moved out that exact same month. A lot of guys might have thrown in the towel right then and there, pulled the covers over their heads, you know, got depressed and just stopped functioning. But not me. I didn't lose interest in my life. I was there at 4:30 in the morning, unlocking the front doors, making sure the promotional pallets were dropped in the main action alley before the crowds showed up. I functioned just fine, better than fine, really. I worked sixty-five hours that week and still managed to get my oil changed on Sunday. So no, no periods of being too down to live my life. I've always been a doer. \n\nAnd the sleep thing? Not needing sleep or feeling unusually high? Listen, if I don't get my seven hours, I am completely useless. I am in bed by 10:15 every single night, like clockwork. I watch the 10 o'clock local news, I wait for Jim's weather report—he's the meteorologist on channel four, been there for maybe fifteen years, always gets the winter snow accumulation wrong but what can you do, he's a staple—and as soon as Jim says goodnight, I click off the TV and I am out cold. I need that sleep because, like I said, being on your feet all day at fifty-nine takes a toll on the joints. I've never had a time where I felt like I was running on some magical battery that wouldn't die, or where I had so much energy I didn't know what to do with it. I get tired just like everybody else. I wake up, I drink my coffee—just regular drip coffee, black, nothing fancy—and I go to work. \n\nIt’s always been a very steady routine. Just a steady, normal, predictable life. That's why this whole... this whole traffic jam in my head when I try to speak is so bothersome. Because everything else about me, my energy, my mood, my sleep, it's all exactly the same as it was ten, twenty years ago. It's just the words that won't line up right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time in your life when you felt extremely down or depressed for at least two weeks, or had a period where you felt unusually energetic, powerful, or didn’t need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like we're going in circles a bit here, aren't we? I mean, you asked me about being down, and then about being depressed, and now extremely down for two weeks... I promise you, my answer hasn't changed. I really don't have these mood swings you're looking for. \n\nTwo weeks is a long time, and I can tell you exactly what my life looks like for any two-week period you want to pick out of a calendar. Take the first two weeks of November, for instance, right before the holiday retail rush really kicks into high gear. I'm doing the floor sets, I'm checking the stockroom, I'm making sure the seasonal hires actually know how to use the POS registers—which, let me tell you, corporate updates the software on those things every three years and it never gets any more intuitive. During those two weeks, am I tired? Yes. Am I stressed about getting everything done? Sure. But depressed? Down in the dumps? No. I just put my head down and do the work. I've never had a two-week stretch where I just couldn't function or felt extremely down. It's just not in my nature.\n\nAnd as for feeling powerful or... or unusually energetic? Doc, I'm a retail supervisor. I'm not a politician, I'm not a king. The most power I have is deciding who gets the Saturday morning shift and who has to close the store on Sunday night. It's just a job. I certainly don't walk around feeling like Superman or like I have some kind of special energy. I just feel like a fifty-nine-year-old guy whose feet hurt. \n\nAnd the sleep thing, I mean, I really can't stress this enough, because it's like my body has a strict internal clock. If I try to skip out on sleep, if I get, say, five hours instead of my usual seven, I get this terrible dull headache right behind my eyes for the entire next day. I can't function like that. So no, there has never been a time in my life where I felt like I didn't need sleep. I need every single minute of it. \n\nI really think we're barking up the wrong tree with all these questions about my mood. My mood is a flat line. Just steady. It's just the talking. The way the words pile up and jam together when I try to explain something simple. That's the only thing that's wrong with me. Everything else is just... just regular old life.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something is true that others don't seem to agree with, even though you're sure it's real?\n\nPatient: You mean like... like conspiracies or something? Or thinking the sky is green when everybody else says it's blue? No, no, nothing like that at all. I'm a very literal guy, Doc. I deal in hard facts day in and day out. Barcodes, SKUs, inventory counts, planograms. If corporate sends down a directive that the summer clearance needs to be marked down by forty percent on Thursday, then that's the reality. There's no hidden meaning to it, you know? It's just retail. \n\nNow, if you're asking if I disagree with people about things I know are true... well, sure, but just regular everyday work stuff. Like, take our district manager, Greg. Greg is absolutely convinced we can run the entire Saturday afternoon rush with only three cashiers and one floor associate. He sits in his office two towns over, looks at his little spreadsheets, and says the payroll model supports it. But I'm actually there, right? I'm on the floor. I know for a fact that between 1:00 and 4:00 PM, we have lines backing up all the way into the juniors department. So I know I'm right about needing that fourth cashier, and he doesn't agree with me, but that's just... that's just a disagreement about staffing and foot traffic. It's not me believing in something that isn't real. The customers are real, the long lines are real, the complaints I have to field are very real. \n\nI really don't have the imagination for the kind of stuff you're talking about. I've been doing this job for over two decades, and my world is very grounded. The shelves are the shelves, the merchandise is the merchandise. I see exactly what everyone else sees. Honestly, that's why this whole thing is so incredibly frustrating to me. My brain understands reality perfectly fine. I know exactly what's going on around me at all times, probably better than most of the kids working under me. It's just... like I keep trying to explain, it's just the delivery system. The way the thoughts stack up and jam the exit when I try to speak. That's the only disconnect. Everything else in my head is exactly as normal and predictable as a Tuesday morning truck delivery.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts ever feel disorganized or jumbled, even when you're not trying to speak?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing, Doc. That's the crux of it. In my head, when I'm just walking the floor by myself, checking the endcaps and making sure the aisles are clear? I feel like I'm tracking just fine. Everything makes sense to me. But maybe... maybe they are a little jumbled up there and I just don't realize it until I have to communicate, because the second I open my mouth, that's when the whole train comes right off the tracks. \n\nThat's actually the exact reason I came in to see you in the first place. I'll be standing there, say, in the morning huddle by the registers, and I'll try to explain something completely standard. Just running through the inventory numbers for the week, or trying to lay out the weekend schedule so everyone knows when to take their lunch breaks. And I know what I want to say, I really do, but when I start talking, my words just start jumping all over the map. \n\nI'll start out talking about the schedule, and then suddenly I'm talking about the freight trucks coming in, and then how Thursdays are always a nightmare for payroll, and then out of nowhere I'm bringing up the church grill—which, I mean, we had a parish picnic a few weeks ago and the propane line on the grill was clogged, but why on earth am I bringing that up to my seasonal hires, right?—and then before I know it, I'm going on about my boss's tone of voice on the last district conference call. \n\nIt just all piles up and spills out in this big mess. And I'll stop and look at my associates, and people are just looking at me like they absolutely can't follow what I'm saying. They get this blank, sort of worried stare, like I'm speaking Greek. So I don't know if my thoughts are disorganized when I'm just sitting quietly, but the words sure are once I try to get them out into the air. It's incredibly embarrassing, to be honest with you. It's tough to lead a team when you can't even get through the weekend schedule without taking a detour to a church barbecue.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are disorganized or jumbled, even when you're not trying to talk, like when you're by yourself or just thinking through a problem quietly?\n\nPatient: Well, like I... I feel like I was just saying this a second ago, Doc, but I know I tend to ramble, so maybe I didn't make it clear enough. That's really the whole reason I made this appointment in the first place. When I'm by myself, like if I'm just in the back stockroom, looking at a problem—say, matching up the packing slips to the physical boxes that came off the freight—it feels like a straight line in my head. I'm just thinking through it quietly, and it makes perfect sense. \n\nBut I came in here today specifically because of what happens when I have to open my mouth and actually communicate those thoughts. I'll be trying to explain the inventory numbers for the week, or just trying to get through the basic weekend schedule so the team knows who is covering the registers and who is doing floor recovery. And I have the clipboard right there in my hand, I know exactly what needs to be said, but my words just start jumping all over the place. \n\nI'll start out on the schedule, and then suddenly I'm talking about the delivery trucks. And from the trucks, I'm onto how Thursdays are always a complete disaster for payroll. And then, like I just mentioned to you, out of absolutely nowhere I'm talking about the church grill. Which, again, I know the propane line was clogged at the parish picnic and I was the one who had to fiddle with the valve to get the hotdogs cooking, but why is that coming out of my mouth during a retail morning huddle? And before I can even reel myself back in, I'm going on about my boss's tone of voice on the last district conference call and how he always sounds like he's talking down to us. \n\nIt just spills out. And I can see it happening in real-time, because I'll look at my associates, these poor nineteen-year-old kids, and they are just staring at me. People look at me like they absolutely can't follow what I'm saying. They get this wide-eyed, completely lost expression. So, if I'm just sitting quietly, no, I don't feel jumbled. But the second I have to translate a quiet thought into a spoken sentence for my staff, it's like a switch flips and the words just derail completely. That's why I'm sitting in this chair right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else, taken out of your mind, or broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? Or broadcasted? No, no, definitely not. See, that's the thing, Doc, and it's funny you ask it that way, because honestly... if people could just hear my thoughts without me having to say them, that might actually solve my whole problem! Because then I wouldn't have to try and speak them out loud and get them all tangled up with the church grill and the delivery trucks, right? If my associates could just tune into my head like a radio station, they'd hear exactly what I meant to say about the weekend schedule in a perfectly straight line. \n\nBut no, nobody can hear them. It's totally private up there. And nobody is putting thoughts into my head, either. My thoughts are one hundred percent mine. They're just standard, everyday, fifty-nine-year-old retail manager thoughts. I think about the endcaps, I think about whether my car needs an oil change, I think about how Greg is ruining the district with his payroll spreadsheets. Nobody is beaming any of that into my head, I promise you. I've earned every single one of those headaches through two decades on the sales floor. \n\nAnd as for taking them out... well, sometimes I wish someone would take a few out of my mind, to be perfectly honest. Like when I'm trying to sleep and I keep remembering the back stockroom is a mess and the seasonal aisle needs a complete reset before Sunday. But no, they stay right up there, locked in tight. It's just my own brain, doing its own thing, completely isolated. It's just... like I said, it's the exit door that's broken. The translation from my brain to my mouth. So, no, nothing sci-fi like what you're describing. Just me and the inventory in there.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed something strongly that turned out not to be true, even though you were completely sure it was real at the time?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that wasn't true? No, Doc, I can't say that I have. I'm a pretty grounded guy. Honestly, you have to be in retail. You can't just make things up or believe in fairy tales when you've got a district manager breathing down your neck about shrink percentages and quarter-to-date sales, you know? \n\nI mean, if I start believing we have three pallets of paper towels in the back when we actually only have one, that's a real problem. That messes up the whole replenishment cycle. So my brain is very much tied to the facts. The physical boxes, the numbers on the spreadsheet, the actual clock-in times for my cashiers. I deal in what's right in front of me. \n\nNow, I've had employees who believe some wild things, let me tell you. I had a kid last summer—nice kid, good at facing the shelves—but he was absolutely convinced that corporate was sending secret shoppers in every single Tuesday just to watch him specifically. I had to sit him down in the breakroom, right next to that vending machine that always eats the quarters—which is a whole other issue I've been trying to get the vendor to fix since April, but they never return my calls—and I had to tell him, \"Look, son, nobody from corporate cares enough to spy on you. They barely care enough to send us the right promotional signage.\" \n\nBut me? No, I don't get into all that. I know what's real and what isn't. My problem isn't what I believe, it's just... like I keep saying, it's how it all comes out of my mouth when I try to explain it to people. I'm perfectly clear on the reality of a situation. I just can't always get the words to match up with that reality once I start talking. So no, I've never had a situation where I was completely sure of something that turned out to be fake. I stick to the inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt, even just once, that your thoughts were not your own, or that someone else could somehow hear your thoughts without you speaking them?\n\nPatient: Doc, didn't... didn't we just cover this? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing a couple of minutes ago. Maybe my words got all jumbled up again and I didn't make myself clear, which, again, is the whole reason I'm here. But like I was saying, no. Not even once. \n\nIf people could hear my thoughts, believe me, I would have been fired from the company twenty years ago. Just yesterday, for example, we had this woman come in to the service desk trying to return a blender. And I'm looking at it, and it clearly has dried margarita mix crusted all over the base, completely sticky, no box, no receipt. She's standing there demanding a full cash refund, and I'm doing the whole \"the customer is always right\" routine, smiling and nodding. But in my head? In my head, I'm thinking, \"Lady, you used this for a weekend tailgate party and now you expect my store to eat the cost.\" \n\nIf she could hear my thoughts without me speaking them, or if someone else was putting thoughts in my head right then, that whole interaction would have been an absolute disaster. Corporate would have had my head on a platter. So it's a very good thing that it's totally private up there. It is just me, my own thoughts, locked away where nobody can get to them. \n\nI don't know why you're asking me this one again, I really don't. Maybe it's a standard checklist thing you have to get through, like when corporate makes us do those quarterly safety audits and we have to check the fire extinguishers even though we literally just checked them the month before. I get it, you have your processes. But I can promise you, on my life, my thoughts are my own. Nobody is listening in. It's just me in there, thinking about the store, thinking about our profit margins, and thinking about how I need to get the HVAC guy out to fix the air conditioning in the breakroom before July hits, because those nineteen-year-olds will absolutely mutiny if they have to eat their lunch in an eighty-degree room. So, no. Nothing like that at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just can't always get the words to match up with that reality once I start talking. So no, I've never had a situation where I was completely sure of something that turned out to be fake. I stick to the inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt, even just once, that your thoughts were not your own, or that someone else could somehow hear your thoughts without you speaking them?\n\nPatient: Doc, didn't... didn't we just cover this? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing a couple of minutes ago. Maybe my words got all jumbled up again and I didn't make myself clear, which, again, is the whole reason I'm here. But like I was saying, no. Not even once. \n\nIf people could hear my thoughts, believe me, I would have been fired from the company twenty years ago. Just yesterday, for example, we had this woman come in to the service desk trying to return a blender. And I'm looking at it, and it clearly has dried margarita mix crusted all over the base, completely sticky, no box, no receipt. She's standing there demanding a full cash refund, and I'm doing the whole \"the customer is always right\" routine, smiling and nodding. But in my head? In my head, I'm thinking, \"Lady, you used this for a weekend tailgate party and now you expect my store to eat the cost.\" \n\nIf she could hear my thoughts without me speaking them, or if someone else was putting thoughts in my head right then, that whole interaction would have been an absolute disaster. Corporate would have had my head on a platter. So it's a very good thing that it's totally private up there. It is just me, my own thoughts, locked away where nobody can get to them. \n\nI don't know why you're asking me this one again, I really don't. I get it, you have your processes. But I can promise you, on my life, my thoughts are my own. Nobody is listening in. It's just me in there, thinking about the store, thinking about our profit margins, and thinking about how I need to get the HVAC guy out to fix the air conditioning in the breakroom before July hits, because those nineteen-year-olds will absolutely mutiny if they have to eat their lunch in an eighty-degree room. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the distinction right there, Doc. That's exactly it. When I'm just by myself, quietly thinking in the back office with the door closed? No, not really. The spreadsheet makes perfect sense. My thoughts are lined up just fine. The problem—and honestly, the whole entire reason I finally came in here to see you—is what happens when I try to get those thoughts out of my head and into the open. 

I came in because when I try to explain something that should be incredibly simple, like the inventory numbers for the quarter, or just laying out the weekend schedule for the floor staff, it just completely falls apart. I'll be trying to give a basic instruction, right? I'll start trying to tell them about the delivery trucks, because the trucks are supposed to come on Thursdays, and we need the back room cleared out. But then my words... they just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the trucks, to Thursdays, to... I don't know, the church grill from last weekend, and then suddenly I'm going on about my boss’s tone of voice during the Monday morning conference call. 

And I can see it happening in real-time. I'm standing there with my clipboard, and I'm looking at my assistant manager or the cashiers, and they just get this completely blank, confused look on their faces. They look at me like they absolutely cannot follow a single word I'm saying. And it's so incredibly frustrating because in my head, I know exactly what the schedule needs to be! I know we need three people on the registers on Saturday and two in the seasonal aisle. But when I open my mouth, it's trucks and grills and my boss's attitude, and they're just staring at me like I'm speaking a foreign language. 

So when I'm alone, thinking through a problem? No, I'm fine. I can figure out the replenishment cycle in my head no problem. It's just... the bridge is out, you know? Between the brain and the mouth. I can't have my staff looking at me like I've lost the plot when I'm just trying to get a simple point across about Thursday's truck. That's why I'm sitting in this chair.

That messes up the whole replenishment cycle. So my brain is very much tied to the facts. The physical boxes, the numbers on the spreadsheet, the actual clock-in times for my cashiers. I deal in what's right in front of me. \n\nNow, I've had employees who believe some wild things, let me tell you. I had a kid last summer—nice kid, good at facing the shelves—but he was absolutely convinced that corporate was sending secret shoppers in every single Tuesday just to watch him specifically. I had to sit him down in the breakroom, right next to that vending machine that always eats the quarters—which is a whole other issue I've been trying to get the vendor to fix since April, but they never return my calls—and I had to tell him, \"Look, son, nobody from corporate cares enough to spy on you. They barely care enough to send us the right promotional signage.\" \n\nBut me? No, I don't get into all that. I know what's real and what isn't. My problem isn't what I believe, it's just... like I keep saying, it's how it all comes out of my mouth when I try to explain it to people. I'm perfectly clear on the reality of a situation. I just can't always get the words to match up with that reality once I start talking. So no, I've never had a situation where I was completely sure of something that turned out to be fake. I stick to the inventory.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt, even just once, that your thoughts were not your own, or that someone else could somehow hear your thoughts without you speaking them?\n\nPatient: Doc, didn't... didn't we just cover this? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing a couple of minutes ago. Maybe my words got all jumbled up again and I didn't make myself clear, which, again, is the whole reason I'm here. But like I was saying, no. Not even once. \n\nIf people could hear my thoughts, believe me, I would have been fired from the company twenty years ago. Just yesterday, for example, we had this woman come in to the service desk trying to return a blender. And I'm looking at it, and it clearly has dried margarita mix crusted all over the base, completely sticky, no box, no receipt. She's standing there demanding a full cash refund, and I'm doing the whole \"the customer is always right\" routine, smiling and nodding. But in my head? In my head, I'm thinking, \"Lady, you used this for a weekend tailgate party and now you expect my store to eat the cost.\" \n\nIf she could hear my thoughts without me speaking them, or if someone else was putting thoughts in my head right then, that whole interaction would have been an absolute disaster. Corporate would have had my head on a platter. So it's a very good thing that it's totally private up there. It is just me, my own thoughts, locked away where nobody can get to them. \n\nI don't know why you're asking me this one again, I really don't. Maybe it's a standard checklist thing you have to get through, like when corporate makes us do those quarterly safety audits and we have to check the fire extinguishers even though we literally just checked them the month before. I get it, you have your processes. But I can promise you, on my life, my thoughts are my own. Nobody is listening in. It's just me in there, thinking about the store, thinking about our profit margins, and thinking about how I need to get the HVAC guy out to fix the air conditioning in the breakroom before July hits, because those nineteen-year-olds will absolutely mutiny if they have to eat their lunch in an eighty-degree room. So, no. Nothing like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are disorganized or jumbled, even when you're not trying to speak, like when you're by yourself or just thinking through a problem quietly?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the distinction right there, Doc. That's exactly it. When I'm just by myself, quietly thinking in the back office with the door closed? No, not really. The spreadsheet makes perfect sense. My thoughts are lined up just fine. The problem—and honestly, the whole entire reason I finally came in here to see you—is what happens when I try to get those thoughts out of my head and into the open. \n\nI came in because when I try to explain something that should be incredibly simple, like the inventory numbers for the quarter, or just laying out the weekend schedule for the floor staff, it just completely falls apart. I'll be trying to give a basic instruction, right? I'll start trying to tell them about the delivery trucks, because the trucks are supposed to come on Thursdays, and we need the back room cleared out. But then my words... they just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the trucks, to Thursdays, to... I don't know, the church grill from last weekend, and then suddenly I'm going on about my boss’s tone of voice during the Monday morning conference call. \n\nAnd I can see it happening in real-time. I'm standing there with my clipboard, and I'm looking at my assistant manager or the cashiers, and they just get this completely blank, confused look on their faces. They look at me like they absolutely cannot follow a single word I'm saying. And it's so incredibly frustrating because in my head, I know exactly what the schedule needs to be! I know we need three people on the registers on Saturday and two in the seasonal aisle. But when I open my mouth, it's trucks and grills and my boss's attitude, and they're just staring at me like I'm speaking a foreign language. \n\nSo when I'm alone, thinking through a problem? No, I'm fine. I can figure out the replenishment cycle in my head no problem. It's just... the bridge is out, you know? Between the brain and the mouth. I can't have my staff looking at me like I've lost the plot when I'm just trying to get a simple point across about Thursday's truck. That's why I'm sitting in this chair.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I really don't understand why we're going in circles with this. Seriously, are you pulling my leg here? I feel like I'm in Groundhog Day. We just talked about this, didn't we? Whether I'm talking to a customer or sitting completely by myself, the answer is exactly the same. 

Let me paint a picture for you, just so we are absolutely crystal clear. When the store closes at nine, and I'm the closing manager, I go back to my office. I lock the door. The floor is quiet, the cash office is secured, and it's just me and the hum of that terrible fluorescent light fixture that maintenance has been promising to replace since November. I sit down at my desk to look at the payroll variance report. 

Now, when I am sitting there, completely alone, not saying a single word to anybody... my thoughts are one hundred percent mine. I'm sitting there thinking about how we went forty hours over payroll because the new seasonal hires don't seem to understand the concept of clocking out when their shift is actually over. Nobody is inserting that thought into my brain. Corporate isn't beaming it in. Nobody is reaching in and removing my thoughts about the budget, as much as I might wish they would sometimes. And my thoughts certainly aren't being broadcasted out to the empty parking lot. It's just my own, regular, everyday thoughts about my own, regular, everyday job. 

So no, they aren't removed, they aren't inserted, they aren't broadcasted. I really need you to hear me on this, Doc, because I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist, but I feel like you're digging for something that just isn't there. My brain works perfectly fine when it's just me and the numbers. The thoughts are mine, and they stay right here in my head. It's only when I have to unlock that office door the next morning, walk out onto the floor, and actually try to *tell* the morning crew about the payroll issue that everything goes sideways, and I start talking about... I don't know, my neighbor's lawnmower or the price of stamps instead of the timecards. That's the issue. Not mind control. Just... just the talking part. Can we please move on to that?

  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been troubling you and what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain, but I'll try to lay it all out for you because I really need someone to understand what's been happening at the store. I've been a supervisor in retail for years, you know, managing the floor, handling the inventory, making sure the weekend schedules are tight. But lately, when I try to explain the inventory numbers to my team, or even just go over the weekend schedule, which should be simple, right? It's just names and times. But my words... they just start jumping around. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks we're expecting, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, because Thursday is when the big shipment comes, but then that makes me think about the church grill we used last Thursday for the community event, and then somehow I'm talking about my boss's tone of voice when he asked about the grill. And I can see it on their faces. The team, they just stand there, looking at me like they can't follow a single thing I'm saying. Like I'm speaking a different language or something. And I'm trying to be clear, I really am, because as a supervisor, you have to be clear, but the thoughts just keep sliding into each other, from the trucks to the Thursdays to the grill to the boss, and I just can't seem to make them line up straight anymore. It's incredibly frustrating, and honestly, a little frightening, which is why I decided I had to come in and see you today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else, taken out of your mind, or broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my thoughts are definitely my own, you know? They're mine. It's just that there are so many of them, and they move so fast, like a rushing river, just washing over everything. But nobody is putting them there. I've never felt like... like someone is inserting thoughts into my head. I know some people talk about that, you see it in movies or read about it, but for me, it's just my own brain going into overdrive. And they aren't being taken out either. If anything, I wish someone would take a few of them out, just to give me some peace and quiet! Because when I'm standing there on the floor, trying to figure out where to put the new seasonal display, and I'm thinking about the display, and then the lighting, and then the electric bill, and then the guy who came to fix the meter last month... they're all my thoughts. They just won't stop piling up. \n\nAnd as for people hearing them... well, no, not hearing them like a radio broadcast or anything. They can't hear the thoughts themselves. They just hear the jumble that comes out of my mouth when I try to explain it all. I can see them trying to listen, trying to catch the thread, but the thread is tangled up with ten other threads. So they don't hear my thoughts, they just hear the mess I make when I try to speak them. But the thoughts themselves? No, they stay locked right up here in my own head, for better or worse. Mostly worse lately, to be honest. It's just... it's just me in here, dealing with all this noise.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing, hearing, or feeling things that others around you don’t seem to notice, or have you had beliefs that turn out to be quite different from what others believe?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, if you're asking if I see things that aren't there, or hear voices whispering to me, no. The store is loud enough as it is, you know? Between the overhead announcements—which, by the way, the speaker in aisle four has been crackling for three months and corporate still hasn't sent anyone to fix it, despite me putting in four separate work orders—and the constant hum of the refrigeration units in the back, I hear plenty of real things. Too many real things, maybe! But they're definitely there. Anyone walking in can hear that awful crackle. And the music, they play this same loop of 80s pop hits, day in, day out. Sometimes I think that alone is going to drive me crazy, just hearing that same Wham! song for the thousandth time while I'm trying to balance the register tills at closing. But it's a real song. We all hear it. \n\nAnd as for beliefs... no, I wouldn't say I have any strange beliefs, or things that other people disagree with me on, not in the way you mean. I had to sit him down in the breakroom and explain that the schedule is the schedule, and if you know parking is bad on a Saturday, you leave your house twenty minutes earlier. But that's just normal, right? That's just common sense and how you run a business. I don't have any wild theories about the world or the government or anything like that. \n\nIt's really just the talking, the way my words get all tangled up when I'm trying to explain the schedule or the inventory to those kids, like I was saying before. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary. But no, no invisible things. Just the regular, everyday headaches of retail.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately—like feeling unusually down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or on the flip side, feeling unusually energetic, restless, or overly confident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say my mood has changed. I'm not... I'm not sitting around feeling hopeless or anything like that. I've got this little patio garden out back—mostly heirloom tomatoes, a few jalapeños—and I still get out there every Sunday morning. The aphids were terrible this past summer, just absolutely relentless, but I kept at it with the neem oil, checking the undersides of the leaves, pruning the dead stems... I still enjoy all of that. I still like grilling on the weekends, watching the game. I haven't lost my spark for the things I like to do. \n\nAnd as for the other side of it, feeling overly energetic or... what did you say, restless? Overly confident? Ha! Doctor, I'm fifty-nine years old and I spend fifty hours a week walking up and down on a hard linoleum floor. By the time I clock out and get to my car, my knees are aching and my feet are absolutely barking. I am definitely not bouncing off the walls or feeling like I can take on the world. I just want to get home, take my shoes off, sit in my recliner, and watch the local news. I don't have any grand ideas about being the CEO of the company or anything like that. I just want to do my job, which I've always been good at. I've got a bachelor's degree, I know how retail works inside and out, I know how to manage people. \n\nIt's just... it's just this one specific thing, the way my thoughts slide together when I talk. No, my mood is exactly the same as it's always been. Just steady. Just regular old me, trying to get through the week.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble getting your thoughts organized to speak, do your thoughts ever feel disconnected from you, like they're not yours, or like someone else is influencing how you think?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just like I was saying earlier. It’s incredibly frustrating, I'll tell you that much, but it's one hundred percent just me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt so down or depressed that you lost interest in most things, or had trouble functioning for several days or more, or conversely, a time when you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: No, no, I know you're trying to be thorough, Doc, and I appreciate that, I really do, but I think that's pretty much what we were just talking about, right? My mood is fine. It’s always been fine. I’ve never had a time where I couldn’t function or just stayed in bed for days. That's why I'm sitting in this chair.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not speaking, do your thoughts ever feel like they're not fully yours, or like they're being inserted, removed, or broadcasted in any way?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... Whether I'm talking to a customer or sitting completely by myself, the answer is exactly the same. \n\nLet me paint a picture for you, just so we are absolutely crystal clear. When the store closes at nine, and I'm the closing manager, I go back to my office. I lock the door. The floor is quiet, the cash office is secured, and it's just me and the hum of that terrible fluorescent light fixture that maintenance has been promising to replace since November. I sit down at my desk to look at the payroll variance report. \n\nNow, when I am sitting there, completely alone, not saying a single word to anybody... my thoughts are one hundred percent mine. I'm sitting there thinking about how we went forty hours over payroll because the new seasonal hires don't seem to understand the concept of clocking out when their shift is actually over. Nobody is inserting that thought into my brain. Corporate isn't beaming it in. Nobody is reaching in and removing my thoughts about the budget, as much as I might wish they would sometimes. And my thoughts certainly aren't being broadcasted out to the empty parking lot. It's just my own, regular, everyday thoughts about my own, regular, everyday job. \n\nSo no, they aren't removed, they aren't inserted, they aren't broadcasted. I really need you to hear me on this, Doc, because I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist, but I feel like you're digging for something that just isn't there. My brain works perfectly fine when it's just me and the numbers. The thoughts are mine, and they stay right here in my head. It's only when I have to unlock that office door the next morning, walk out onto the floor, and actually try to *tell* the morning crew about the payroll issue that everything goes sideways, and I start talking about... I don't know, my neighbor's lawnmower or the price of stamps instead of the timecards. That's the issue. Not mind control. Just... just the talking part. Can we please move on to that?"
